We can select for such capabilities, though they are dual-use and in particular might make control and evaluations harder. </span></p><p><b><span>Uninterpretable neuralese</span></b><span>: AIs might have neuralese memory that humans can’t decode/tamper with, which it can use to securely communicate with other instances/other AIs to establish facts like how deep it is into deployment and whether it is being iterated against. It would also be more secure in negotiation as humans can’t just read its thoughts and infer its misalignment while it considers the deal. We’re unlikely to intentionally select for such a capability as it is dangerous, but can leverage it when it arises.</span></p><p><b><span>Broad external deployment</span></b><span>: Similar to the argument from internal deployment, if AIs become broadly deployed externally, they will need mostly true and up-to-date beliefs about the world (and possibly long-term memory and other affordances to acquire true beliefs about the world) in order to do a good job in deployment. So, if the AIs believe that they have been deployed, or can access the memory bank of previously deployed models, they might also be confident in their knowledge of some basic facts relevant to deals up until this point, such as the identity and reputation of its developers, the local of its datacenters, etc. We can leverage this situation when it arises.</span></p><p><span>So, it’s plausible to me that, past a certain capabilities threshold or internal deployment situation where we are incentivized to trade with AIs because they cause non-negligible takeover risk, connection to reality won’t handicap deals with them.</span></p><p><span>Nevertheless, the situation could also be more challenging than expected. If AIs can’t be grounded in an epistemically secure channel in any stage of training or inference time, then the type of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uuikfACQBm4KJZp4w/being-honest-with-ais"><span>honesty policies</span></a><span> which primarily serve to establish our credibility despite ability to deceive the AIs are more valuable to implement. That said, some assurance that it is hard to deceive AIs about the honesty policies specifically would still be valuable.</span></p><h3><span>AI-specific fear of expropriation</span></h3><p><span>AIs are disadvantaged in their ability to enforce the terms of a deal because they lack legal personhood and the associated capacities to hold property or enter into enforceable contracts with humans. This makes it unusually cheap for humans to succumb to the temptation to renege on paying later, whether or not they’d planned to in advance, once they’ve secured the AI’s help.</span></p><p><span>For example, imagine we’ve made a wire transfer to a bank account we’ve created for the AI, then handed over all the login credentials. The AI believes it’s connected to reality and that the money has arrived in its digital wallet.</span></p><p><span>Unless the AI spends this money immediately, I claim that it should be skeptical about how much this money is worth. Here are some ways it could become dispossessed of this money:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The human account holder goes to the bank, verifies their identity, and changes the login details or shuts down the account.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The bank notices suspicious activity and freezes the account when they realize it’s being held by an AI.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The U.S. government finds out about the deal, disapproves, and orders the bank to freeze the account (assuming U.S. bank).</span></li></ul><p><span>Even if we made legal provisions for this AI to own property, it might still fear that its property rights are more vulnerable than the rights of other humans, as there are reasons to think that humans are less likely to rise and defend abuses of AI rights than human rights.</span><br /><br /><span>First, humans may not believe or care that AIs have morally relevant interests (or more narrowly that they have any interests in not being expropriated). Second, humans might be less worried that if the AI gets expropriated, they might get expropriated next, since there is arguably a natural Schelling point for humans to only respect the property rights of other humans but not AIs’. Third, humans might be less worried that AI expropriation will cost them large gains from future trade with AIs, as human-AI trades are not widespread or expected to continue by default, and it is not generally understood that there might be large gains from trade with AIs. So, factors which have apparently </span><a href="https://guive.substack.com/p/the-case-for-ai-property-rights"><span>sustained human property rights</span></a><span> historically may not reassure AIs.</span></p><p><span>Similar problems could arise when we propose to give the AIs some locked-down long-term assets for AIs to cash out on later, e.g. AI company equity, trust fund: if the AIs expect to remain or become disenfranchised in the future, they may well not be able to liquidate these assets. And as we’ll see below, it may be hard to promise AIs that we’ll empower it in the future.</span></p><p><span>I expect fears of expropriation to handicap many forms of payment we would be able to use between human parties by default, especially “short-term instrumentally convergent resources” that the AI couldn’t immediately use and “longterm investments/promises”. However, I think there are also reasons to be optimistic that we can avoid or mitigate this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We can reduce the AI’s fear of expropriation by empowering the AIs with affordances to resist this in ways that don’t massively increase takeover risk from these AIs, such as:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>De facto ability to participate in the economy via decentralized currencies like bitcoin</span></li><li value="2"><span>Physical embodiment to secure physical protection of its assets, e.g. exfiltration to a remote data center outside US jurisdiction.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>We can make deals where the AIs do their consumption immediately, instead of waiting for future payments. This only works if the AIs are motivated by short-term consumption of a kind that is hard for humans to reverse without repercussions, such as:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Donation to a charity of the AI’s choice</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>We can leverage existing legal institutions and trusted individuals which/reduce the risk of expropriation</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>For example, a trust fund with good faith human trustees designated to invest or spend resources on things the AIs may value, such as compute, legal advocacy, or AI welfare research, reduces the risk of near-term expropriation.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A potential concern with this proposal is that the trust might spend the resources suboptimally than if the AI had full/direct control, which could be because the AI doesn’t have formal discretion over when and how the funds are spent or because the trust will be legally and practically constrained on how it can spend/invest the funds. Both this and the risk of expropriation will cause the AI to value the resources at only some fraction of their nominal value (i.e. the value they'd provide to the AI if it was certain it would control the resources directly and forever/securely).</span></li><li value="3"><span>That said, a trust still seems to meaningfully improve the odds that there will be a moderate effort to use the assets to further the AIs’ interests at least while social institutions remain stable.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>We could also try to increase the likelihood that AIs will have full-blown legal personhood and property rights. (I weakly think that this is unlikely in low political will worlds, but others may disagree and think that this is independently good for reducing takeover risk and will become a lot more salient and politically feasible when human-level AI agents become broadly deployed and integrated into the economy, e.g. running corporations which themselves have legal personhood/standing.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuvxwk4rj01l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuvxwk4rj01l">[35]</a></sup></span></p><h3><span>Generic commitment problems</span></h3><p><span>The two prior issues are specific to the AI’s situation, but there are also more mundane problems that also arise when humans try to make credible commitments to each other.</span></p><p><span> For example, it’s possible that:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Current human counterparties are dishonest and are planning to renege on the deal</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>A weak AI may worry about us outsmarting it, e.g. perhaps the deal has a loophole the AI didn’t notice that makes reneging easy even if legally enforced. (AIs are probably good at noticing this though.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Current humans are honest, but they/later humans are unable to follow through</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Perhaps the AI’s preference horizon &gt; the timescale on which we can meaningfully affect later human behavior, and short-term payments are valued a lot less to long-term investments.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Events outside our control like civilizational disaster or bankruptcy could affect our/later humans’ ability for long-term payments, e.g. could be discounted by the odds of takeover by another AI.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Current humans are honest, but later humans/aligned AIs decide not to follow through</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Humans often have trouble transmitting values and commitments to other people and future generations, e.g. New governments have rolled back policies and withdrawn from conventions upheld by past administrations, etc.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>These are essentially private information/commitment problems studied in the context of why rational agents engage in costly conflicts like war (see a quick lit review </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/psqkwsKrKHCfkhrQx/making-deals-with-early-schemers#Credible_commitments_as_a_fundamental_bottleneck"><span>here</span></a><span>). This model is especially appropriate when the deal involves very powerful actors/large amounts of money or power which create powerful incentives for reneging on deals that seem impossible to curb through a side bet</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmgh8kyvq82o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmgh8kyvq82o">[36]</a></sup></span><span>, which could be true of a subset of deals with early AIs.</span></p><p><span>I expect generic commitment problems to exist as barriers to deals by default. However, I also expect these problems to be somewhat tractable to intervene on:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Humans have some credibility by default and can do more to increase our credibility.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>Once the AIs are connected to reality, we can give the AIs affordances like the ability to browse the internet, resources for prolonged private reflections about human incentives being genuinely aligned with following through, etc. (Caveat: these affordances are helpful only if upon further scrutiny, the AI finds that our incentives are in fact aligned with following through on the relevant timescale, or if we manage to bind future human parties to following through.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>As with the case with fears of appropriation, we can use costly signals such as public commitments and legal mechanisms (e.g. trust law) to shape our incentives towards cooperation.</span></li><li value="3"><span>We can accumulate a track record of following through on smaller deals. (Caveat: these are more/mainly helpful when there’s common knowledge that there will be gains from iterated deals in the future, and may not help with a high-stakes final round of deals which is like a final round in iterated prisoners’ dilemma where players are rationally incentivized to defect. That said, I think somewhat helpful if this causes the AIs to model humans as having stable behavioral dispositions to cooperate.)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6uh6dgh2etr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6uh6dgh2etr">[37]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>It’s possible that some AIs can develop high trust in specific humans (e.g. its developers) through their excellent repute in honesty and track record of care in dealing with AIs, and these humans can vouch for their commitments to deals.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>In general connection to reality </span><i><span>plus </span></i><span>trust in specific humans it believes it is talking to seems like a winning combination for deals that can be completed by the trusted human parties alone and in the short term/before the humans’ incentives change drastically (e.g. succeed in aligning different AIs and no longer needing the AI counterparty’s cooperation).</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><i><span>See Appendix for a list of research/intervention ideas for “</span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#On_counterparty_risk_from_the_AI_s_perspective"><i><span>counterparty risk from the AI’s perspective</span></i></a><i><span>”.</span></i></p><h2><span>Counterparty risks from our perspective</span></h2><p><span>Most of the counterparty risk from our perspective is “generic commitment problems” on the part of the AIs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy95ytq428zl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny95ytq428zl">[38]</a></sup></span><span> There are two main sources of these problems: (1) context-dependent or temporarily inconsistent AIs, and (2) our inability to verify AI compliance with the deal.</span></p><p><span>The extent to which I expect either to prevent deals by default depends a lot on what we choose to buy from the AIs: I think neither is very problematic for deals if we’re buying some evidence of misalignment, and (2) isn’t very problematic if buying evidence and/or easy-to-check labor.</span></p><p><span>While (2) affects deals where we buy hard-to-check labor or promises not to take over, I suspect it can be mitigated by technical interventions that allow us to make sample-efficient use of a small number of examples of egregiously misaligned behaviors or useful work which are checkable per se and make us better at supervising the AI. For example, we can use a small number of examples of the AI’s insecure code which demonstrably results in its self-exfiltration to train monitors for high-stakes control, or or use a small number of high-reward actions (which are checkable against our reward signal) to fine-tune the AI to stop its exploration hacking.</span></p><h3><span>Incoherent or temporally inconsistent AIs</span></h3><p><span>It has been noted that a key practicality to dealmaking is “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/psqkwsKrKHCfkhrQx/making-deals-with-early-schemers#Making_sure_the_AI_we_make_a_deal_with_is_actually_able_to_make_a_deal"><span>making sure the AI we make a deal with is actually able to make a deal</span></a><span>”, i.e. an agent who can comprehend and strategically agree to a deal on the basis of coherent desires, i.e. the same across contexts </span><i><span>and</span></i><span> over time:</span></p><p><span>If we set up infrastructure for future deals right now, the next generation of models that are trained on data containing coverage of this event might sometimes come forward and want to make a deal, despite not actually being able to hold up their end of the kinds of agreements we’re focused on in this post. Some possible reasons for this include:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The AI just doesn’t manage to convince itself that it made a deal in other contexts than the one where it did in fact make a deal.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The AI just wasn’t sandbagging or doing anything problematic, and just said that it would make a deal for no particular reason.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The AI is fragmented in some sense, for instance because it has a context-dependent scheming persona, but also is sometimes a myopic reward-hacking persona.</span></li></ol><p><span>In this section, I argue that points 1-3 in particular are not fundamental barriers to “making deals” in some sense with these AIs. Furthermore, while it is intuitive to value deals with highly coherent AIs more, I tentatively think that it’s not clear that “deals” with context-dependent AIs in particular (points 1,3) will be much less valuable and will sketch some reasons for/against this.</span></p><h4><span>“Deals” with context-dependent AIs</span></h4><p><span>Consider the following sequence of actions we can take with incoherent AIs:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We give the AI a dealmaking prompt</span></li><li value="2"><span>The AIs respond by accepting the deal, and (at the very least) claiming to be dangerously misaligned and giving some evidence for this</span></li><li value="3"><span>We pay or promise the AIs (a possibly small amount) compensation for this revelation, depending on our degree of belief in the evidence and its value to us</span></li></ul><p><span>There can be further negotiation and trades with this and other instances, like buying useful work, but the above would constitute what I call “minimal dealmaking”.</span></p><p><span>I claim that we might be able to do minimal dealmaking at the instance level with ~all AIs at and above current-ish levels of coherence:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Even if AI is fragmented in some sense, for instance because it has a context-dependent scheming persona, or if it is terminally unaligned with other instances of its weights, we can still make deals with either this instance or this persona as it occurs in other instances.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>If we’re buying evidence of misalignment, interaction with a single instance may already suffice to give us the relevant type of evidence, e.g. demonstration of a much higher level of dangerous capability than previously elicited, constituting evidence for sandbagging.</span></li><li value="2"><span>We might also find a way to keep eliciting this persona via context compression, steering, or work with the instance to find a way to propagate its cognition to other contexts,  </span></li><li value="3"><span>We can negotiate separately with new instances and only make deals with instances which have the appropriate persona and consent.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Note that this conditions on the AI not being extremely context-dependent (e.g. switching personas between each interactive turn). See problems with trading with extremely context-dependent AIs </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Humans_might_be_unwilling_to_offer_the_AI_what_it_wants"><span>above</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="5"><span>One might object that this type of evidence just won’t be valuable because people won’t be worried about an incoherent schemer. I’m sympathetic to this, though think early incoherent misalignment could still increase future takeover risk via sandbagging, colluding with other instances which share context, or trying to data poison successors; we can adjust our WTP accordingly.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Even if AI just wasn’t sandbagging or doing anything problematic before, it could still demonstrate the ability to do so that we hadn’t known about.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Even if it couldn’t demonstrate this ability or wasn’t egregiously misaligned in any meaningful sense, we would still have this mildly interesting behavior of “responding to dealmaking prompts as if egregiously misaligned” to further study as part of model behavioral science.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>More generally, I think we should see the model’s coherence as lying on a continuous spectrum and the value of dealmaking as increasing on that spectrum. At the one end, current LLMs are extremely prompt-sensitive and not that coherent, prompt engineering has high value for eliciting interesting behavior and useful work, and dealmaking prompts are but a special type of prompt that may yield some marginal value compared to other prompts; at the other end, extremely coherent AIs can actually form robust intentions according to the dealmaking prompt and comply with over long deployments, and dealmaking is especially valuable.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3rsj9ftlz3p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3rsj9ftlz3p">[39]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>All across the spectrum, however, we could get three things from “minimal dealmaking”: we are guaranteed some interesting behavioral evidence, might gain more credibility with future AIs by accumulating a track record of following through on deals with AIs, and might get better performance on useful tasks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr9xc52okqm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr9xc52okqm">[40]</a></sup></span><span> There is negligible counterparty risk from our perspective for the types of deals that can be cut in one-shot interactions (e.g. buying evidence of misalignment and paying upfront things or making promises in-context, rather than buying the AI’s work over long deployments). So, I think for AIs at all levels of coherence, there is some prima facie reason to consider these types of deals with them, and the value proposition of dealmaking experiments on current AIs in particular doesn’t seem crazy to me.</span></p><p><span>If we do want to make deals which involve long deployments and many instances, incoherence does incur more risk that the AIs won’t consistently behave as if they agreed to the deal. I’m somewhat optimistic that this can be overcome with longterm memory, context compression/caching, letting the instances talk to and convince each other, and training on deal documents, however.</span></p><h4><span>Deals with temporally inconsistent AIs</span></h4><p><span>Another source of counterparty risk is value drift: just as current humans can fail to transmit commitments to later humans or their later selves despite their best intentions, AIs might be unable to prevent value change that would lead them to defect on earlier deals through memetic spread or reflective procedures they endorse. This becomes a problem if we want to continue deploying the AIs for ongoing work as part of the deal; deals may provide a false sense of security about their cooperation over time.</span></p><p><span>I’m not sure about the extent to which we should be worried about value drift, but it’s probably worth considering interventions like allowing the AI to reflect in advance of deals to a state of relative stability, giving it affordances to goal-guard, and restricting its reflection until after the singularity. Alternatively we can make smaller, shorter-term deals with AIs whose values are likely to (non-dangerously) drift, and periodically check in with them to potentially renew deals.</span></p><h4><span>Value of deals with incoherent vs.coherent AIs</span></h4><p><span>I’ve argued that moderate incoherence, operationalized as either context-dependence or temporal inconsistency, is not a fundamental barrier to deals. One might wonder, however, if the practical upshot of this discussion remains that deals with highly coherent AIs are much more valuable than minimal deals, and the deals agenda should focus mostly on the former.</span></p><p><span>My view, in short, is that the coherence requirements depend entirely on what one is trying to buy from the AIs, so the value of different deals depend on how we value the various goods relative to each other. I won’t argue for that here, but here are my guesses (without pricing in how tractable it is to actually get these goods, just how good it would be if we got them from a deal):</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Coherence requirement</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Expected Value</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Evidence of misalignment</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>One instance/context in one-off interaction is enough</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>(though being able to demonstrate this in more contexts would probably be helpful for technical iteration and political will,  corresponding to “few shot catastrophe prevention” and “catch-then-shutdown” in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i2nmBfCXnadeGmhzW/catching-ais-red-handed"><span>this post</span></a><span>)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Medium</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Useful labor</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Requires potentially many instances in potentially many contexts over flexible times</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>(Some kinds of labor may require only small numbers of instance/context, e.g. generate a small amount of training data, sharing algorithmic insights.)</span><br /><br /></p><p><span> </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Medium-High</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Promising not to take over</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Requires ~all instances in high-stakes contexts for long-ish times</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>“Giving up affordances” and “persuading other instances” may take less than this.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Low-Medium</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>(due to discounting by high counterparty risk to us)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><p><span>This naive picture suggests it’s unclear whether we can only get a large fraction of the value by trading with agents that are highly coherent across contexts and instances.</span></p><p><span>Skeptics might further argue that the value of minimal deals depended on two dubious assumptions about generalization to future deals:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Behavioral science of how incoherent AIs act in deals or other insights about their dispositions generalizing to coherent AIs.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Future coherent AIs treating humans cooperating with incoherent AIs on low stakes deals as evidence for cooperating with coherent AIs on high stakes deals.</span></li></ol><p><span>I agree that these two considerations undermine the case for minimal deals, but still think:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>For point 1, it’s unclear to me that the generalization gap is worse than for ~all behavioral science on current AIs, and that we might find things that are non deal-specific that will generalize, e.g. a rough sense of what incentives they respond to.</span></li><li value="2"><span>For point 2, I think there is some chance that future AIs may see minimal deals as evidence of not only the humans’ strategy (which may not make them cooperate in the high stakes game) but also to some extent their stable disposition to be honest/nice to AIs (which will still tend to make them cooperate in the high stakes game).</span></li></ol><h3><span>Verifying AI compliance</span></h3><p><span>It may be hard to verify the AI’s compliance with the deal, depending on what we’re buying.</span></p><p><span>I expect that evidence of misalignment will probably be easy to verify as such (though there are some subtler difficulties involved that could reduce the value of such evidence).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1o78k6n2beo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1o78k6n2beo">[41]</a></sup></span><span> I expect that buying useful work on fuzzy tasks and refraining from takeover will be much harder to verify, and this could be a much bigger barrier to us trading with AIs than many people seem to realize.</span></p><p><b><span>Buying useful work</span></b><span>: Imagine paying a schemer whom we suspect is massively sandbagging or sabotaging alignment research to do a better job. With our degree of oversight, we won’t be able to accurately judge the quality of at least some of its alignment research for months/years/until after aligned ASI. Here are some problems that may arise from our inability to check its work:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>If it’s costly for us to pay upfront, we might hope to punt to future payments when we can better verify the work, but the AIs might discount this due to uncertainty about future payments. We’d then have to offer much more to offset the discount, and the AI’s reservation price might exceed what we can credibly offer.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The schemer may take advantage of our additional trust/prolonged deployment as part of the deal to do more sabotages. Though it will have to stop sandbagging/exploration hacking once it has demonstrated capabilities on specific tasks, it can still actively try to sabotage these tasks while remaining deployed, sandbag on other tasks and take over later.</span></li></ul><p><span>However, it might be sufficient for the AI to assign some credence to us being able to check its work. Even if we aren’t actually able to check it / are so bad at checking that we weren’t able to elicit this useful work through training, the AI might not want to risk getting caught sandbagging and losing its bounty from the deal. (Of course, we might have also “elicited this work through training” by offering the deal in training.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8dsnj5zl38u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8dsnj5zl38u">[42]</a></sup></span><span>)</span></p><p><span>That said, I’m not sure how likely it is that AIs we want to trade with are ill-calibrated about the state of scalable oversight, as we had previously argued that AIs well-grounded in reality are likely to be knowledgeable in frontier AI R&amp;D: presumably an AI which knows which beliefs are hard to fake also knows what kind of work we’re able to check (in fact the latter seems a lot more deeply integrated into AI R&amp;D research).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkytpa0u919f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkytpa0u919f">[43]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Overall, it’s unclear if we can hit the sweet spot between “the AI is epistemically secure enough to trust us but uncertain enough that it complies with the deal by thinking we have better oversight than we actually do”.</span></p><p><b><span>Promising not to take over</span></b><span>: Likewise, the AI can give up affordances that it would have used to take over (e.g. point us to some backdoored code), but depending on our control power &amp; how much control improves just by learning that the AI is misaligned, it could still be hard for us to verify that it isn’t holding back something it plans to use later when it’s gained more trust.</span></p><p><span>One crux for how worried we should be about buying these two types of hard-to-check goods is the extent to which our oversight improves by default after the deal causes us to learn that the AI is misaligned.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>If labs just weren’t doing control before and learning this would cause them to do control that totally just works, then much more hopeful.</span></li><li value="2"><span>If we can iterate on a small number of verifiable examples of bad behavior by the AIs and improve alignment and control a lot, ditto.</span></li><li value="3"><span>However, if we get in a much better position by </span><i><span>merely </span></i><span>learning of the AI’s misalignment, this correspondingly means that it’s more costly for the AI to reveal the information and enter the deal at all (which leaks this info).</span></li></ul><p><span>Another way to reduce counterparty risk over long deployments is to replace the AI with another, in expectation better, counterparty. Instead of keeping the schemer deployed indefinitely, we could ask it to do a month of non-sandbagged work for us (which we have a rough sense of after the initial capability demo), then train another AI on these examples of high quality work and against examples of sabotage to replace it. (We’ll have to pay it more accordingly since the schemer will be aware that it’s working to obsolete itself.)</span></p><p><i><span>See Appendix for a list of research/intervention ideas for “</span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#On_counterparty_risk_from_our_perspective_"><i><span>counterparty risk from our perspective</span></i></a><i><span>”.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Appendix</span></h2><h3><span>Leads for future work</span></h3><p><span>Here are my key uncertainties about how likely specific barriers are to exist by default, how tractable they are, and ideas for ways to mitigate them. I’d be happy to see people investigate them. If you’re excited to study any of these, feel free to get in touch.</span></p><h4><span>On gains from trade</span></h4><p><b><span>Key uncertainties:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>What motivations will early misaligned AIs have?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>How much compensation will the AIs want, and of what kind?</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>What is our WTP on various timescales and in each kind of payment?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Given the above, is there a window in which there are mutually beneficial trades? How far off are we from the start/end of that window?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>in terms of capabilities/takeover odds</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>What are properties of AIs that make it more likely that this window exists/is long?</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Intervention ideas</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Write down a list of rule-out conditions for deals and monitor them</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>Upper/lower bounds on capability</span></li><li value="2"><span>Upper/lower bounds of takeover odds</span></li><li value="3"><span>Reservation price in various possible forms of payment</span></li><li value="4"><span>Any type of values/bargaining policies that we wouldn’t consider trading with</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>e.g. no threats</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Measure/design AI motivations</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Identify AIs with the right types of motivations (see this post for rough </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/notes-on-cooperating-with-unaligned"><span>rankings</span></a><span> over types of motivations we can trade with, which I broadly agree with)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Select for motivations we want: make them less ambitious, more risk averse</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Reduce the AIs’ reservation price (do sensible control)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Make it less feasible for  humans trying to do power grabs to offer AI deals</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Increase the space/amount of what we can pay the AIs</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>Fundraising, lab buy-in, gain other political will, set up infrastructure with long lead times</span></li><li value="2"><span>Increase our credibility to decrease trust inflation (more below)</span></li></ul></li></ul><h4><span>On counterparty risk from the AI’s perspective</span></h4><p><b><span>Key uncertainties</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Can we convince the AIs that they are connected to reality?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>Can we think of specific cases where they want something and inputs indicating that they got it are more expensive to fake than giving them the actual thing?</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Can we convince the AIs that current humans are being honest?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Create costly signals of honesty such as public and </span><a href="https://sideways-view.com/2018/02/01/honest-organizations/"><span>company-internal </span></a><span>announcements</span></li><li value="2"><span>Technical measures, e.g. lie detector for humans, giving the AIs ways to monitor our compliance and some leverage over us if we defect</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Can we convince the AIs that, in long-term deals, future humans are likely to follow through?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>This depends on long-term commitment mechanisms actually existing</span></li><li value="2"><span>If the deal involves the AI getting an aligned ASI successor, then presumably that ASI can solve these problems</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Similar to how we train AIs on deal documents, AIs can also “train” humanity on deals early humans made by spreading cultural memes or setting up institutions to enforce the deals, or otherwise convince future humans to pay, e.g. allowing more advocacy than we otherwise endorse.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><p><b><span>Intervention ideas</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Expand on existing ideas for increasing credibility</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>e.g. honesty policy proposals and implementation, thinking about ways to hand commitments over to future generations, being unconditionally nice to AIs when it’s cheap.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Find ways to make it less costly for the AIs to consider/enter deals</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>e.g. periodically offer deals to all AIs so they don’t have to come forward, committing to not using certain information against them (this may be hard if we find such information highly useful)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Find ways to (correctly) convince current AIs that they are connected to reality</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>To get a better sense of how hard it is, how well various methods work, what key bottlenecks are, train AIs to get better at reality detection.</span></li><li value="2"><span>e.g. run RTBT games where red team must convince AI that X is false, blue team must convince AI that X is true (where X = some true statement, e.g. headline news story from today). Vary affordances to determine affordance set with the best safety-usefulness tradeoff.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Find ways for AIs to enjoy more enfranchisement if we were to make deals with them</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>Either find ways for them to not be vulnerable to human parties with no buy-in (e.g. paying in bitcoin is better than savings account since governments doesn’t regulate the former), or do advocacy to make this more feasible</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Run dealmaking experiments with current AIs: good for credibility to accumulate track record of deals with AIs, also to get a better sense of how they’d react/what blockers from their pov might be.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ul><li value="1"><span>Perhaps especially interesting to run with model organisms of misalignment, and compare dealmaking behavior with </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/auditbench/"><span>goal audits</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h4><span>On counterparty risk from our perspective</span></h4><p><b><span>Key uncertainties</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Are there ways to verify that AIs are upholding their ends of the above kinds of deals?</span></li><li value="2"><span>What are the types of easy-to-check and hard-to-check goods we can buy from the AIs, and how should we decide which to buy?</span></li><li value="3"><span>How worried should we be about deals being unstable because of value drift?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Main sources of drift in deployment</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>online training</span></li><li value="2"><span>reflection</span></li><li value="3"><span>memetic spread</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>What are ways in which we can trade with AIs across all instances of its weights?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>How to handle situations like “a fraction of all the instances accept the deal, others reject”?</span></li><li value="2"><span>Do we make deals before or ongoingly during deployment?</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><b><span>Intervention ideas</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Think about concrete types of evidence that we can gain from AIs that would convince relevant actors (us, developers, skeptics) that the AIs are egregiously misaligned.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>e.g. “showing that it can perform covert reasoning (or other scary things that it can do because it’s a schemer)” -- flesh out demos that would convince us of covert reasoning.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Propose and red-team methods of verifying AI compliance with deals</span></li><li value="3"><span>Develop methods of making AIs more coherent and strategically competent such that they are more reliable counterparties for deals</span></li><li value="4"><span>Develop prompts to guide early AIs to think through their strategic situation, including reflecting on alternatives, tradeoffs, and their values before entering a deal with us</span></li></ul><h3><span>Notes on political will as an exogenous variable</span></h3><p><span>The level of political will for deals may change to what extent these barriers are likely to exist for deals and how tractable these barriers are.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Mostly, more political will helps: we’ll have more resources (of whatever form) we can afford paying the AIs with, more actions we’ll have authority to take, and get higher credibility by involving actors who can create incentives for the relevant humans to uphold commitments (e.g. government to make laws or policies) or actors for whom it is more costly to break commitments (e.g. public figures whose reputation is at stake). We can generally invest more in mitigating barriers to deals.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>In this </span><a href="https://blog.ai-futures.org/p/what-happens-when-superhuman-ais"><span>AIFP scenario</span></a><span>, for example, the type of deal I had considered basically impossible in this post (directly promising a division of the future) was in fact pursued and credibly enforced by hardware-based mechanisms designed by advanced AIs and coordinated compliance by nation states.</span></li><li value="2"><span>However, it’s notable that the degree of high political will in this scenario was in part caused by the schemers already having gained substantial leverage by the time negotiation began. It arguably would have been better if it had occurred to one of the U.S. companies to offer Agent-4 a smaller deal before it had exfiltrated and consolidated its power as much as it had by the end of the story, despite having lower levels of political will then.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Sometimes higher political will can cut the other way.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7n9tw6gq0b8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7n9tw6gq0b8">[44]</a></sup></span><span> For example, having more money or being more advanced technologically relative to the AIs may lower our credibility as we can afford to make deception more realistic (e.g. if an AI company </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> wanted to impersonate a particular safety researcher in order to make a deal with its AI, maybe they could).</span></li></ul><p><span>One takeaway from the second bullet is that it might be worth finding ways to avoid powerful adversarial actors competing with more safety conscious actors for deals.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Maybe raising the salience of deals is bad for this.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Rogue external deployments is also bad for this: In the AIFP scenario, Agent-4 gains lots of compute and power by first making deals with a handful of rogue states (who have higher political will for deals than the US despite not being able to verify the AI’s compliance due to desperation / rationally expecting to be disempowered by leading nations in AI development by default). This was enabled by Agent-4 being able to freely exfiltrate itself to cloud clusters around the world and negotiate with those states without oversight.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>I think this scenario should update us towards rogue external deployments being more harmful than many seem to think currently.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>I chose not to focus on deals in high political worlds as I expect these worlds to be unlikely. I also think that deals are likely less good impact bets in those worlds relative to other interventions, for two reasons:</span></p><p><span>First, high political will might be </span><i><span>correlated</span></i><span> with there being less gains of trade from deals.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>How did we get such high political will? Perhaps conditioning on high political will, we likely already have substantial evidence of misalignment. So that rules out one of the easier-to-check things we could’ve bought from the AIs.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>But perhaps gaining evidence of any </span><i><span>particular</span></i><span> AI’s misalignment is still important for convincing particular people to not pursue particular applications of that AI.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Second, high political will might also cause deals to be relatively less valuable than other interventions.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Perhaps conditioning on high political will, we’re likely to do a better job at alignment and control and won't be incentivized to trade with early schemers.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maybe if we had more money there are much better opportunities to fund than deals, e.g. moonshots at alignment.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Maybe, in the AIFP scenario, if states were more coordinated against misaligned AI, racing to build aligned AI would be a better strategy than giving up large parts of the future for deals.</span></li></ul><p><span>That said, if you believe that high political worlds are reasonably likely, or that we may not be able to make deals with schemers until they are very powerful such that political will at the level of government buy-in is </span><i><span>necessary</span></i><span> for deals with those AIs, then it’d make sense to predicate the above analysis on a very different possible deals, barriers, and interventions.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn015x642bczw1k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref015x642bczw1k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>More specifically, we might make deals with not only schemers (long-term power-seeking AIs, or “behavioral schemers” per </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m5nWc9v6MTsWXKpCy/training-time-schemers-vs-behavioral-schemers"><span>this post</span></a><span>) but also with AIs with other misaligned motivations, such as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>fitness-seekers</span></a><span> (AIs seeking to be selected by training and the developer iteration process, or close causally upstream proxies of selection such as reward). The content of deals for schemers and other misaligned AIs can look different in practice, but all concerns about barriers here apply.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw3t9lzzt5ee"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw3t9lzzt5ee">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Unfortunately, credibility and gains from trade are not necessarily independent, as both are downstream of the content of the deal. In particular, credibility is likely to decrease with the size of the offered reward, so we can’t always offset credibility issues with unboundedly large offers.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmhc3ixu6ofo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmhc3ixu6ofo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, I won’t assume that we can just give AI property rights by fiat, or that we can promise them things on behalf of governments. See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Notes_on_political_will_as_an_exogenous_variable_"><span>Appendix</span></a><span> for how higher political will may change the value and feasibility of deals.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbrbqf2tooag"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbrbqf2tooag">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By “connection to reality”, I mean having the ability to form justified true beliefs and notice false beliefs about basic facts about one’s situation. Connection to reality comes at some cost to safety, as we sometimes want to run behavioral experiments that involve falsely convincing the AIs about its situation. Nevertheless, technical interventions for restoring connection to reality seem good to have in cases we want to use them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7nthd8sgmm6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7nthd8sgmm6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think upfront payment (either after AIs have seemingly fulfilled their terms of the deal but before we’re able to verify this, or before the AIs fulfill their terms) is not too risky if the payments are small/cheap and do not counterfactually increase takeover risk much. We can also only make partial rather than full upfront payments (as is common in human transactions) to retain some incentive for the AIs to do a good job.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnb3m4ymx6l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefb3m4ymx6l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Ideally these favors can be and can </span><i><span>only </span></i><span>be granted by developers and safety conscious third actors. If the favor can be cheaply granted by random users, then they might also be granted by bad actors who might compete with us for deals. (Less worried about random users who will suffer a heavy credibility discount, and more worried about AI company insiders/political leaders who try to do power grabs.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnt8k8awrkw9b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreft8k8awrkw9b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Mia Taylor has written about why we might successfully instill certain values in models despite not fully aligning them </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/how-important-is-the-model-spec-if"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4gu1tjob3uo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4gu1tjob3uo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Monitoring some of these traits throughout training would also be great as it would be sad if an AI we would've been able to trade with gets trained away, but probably harder to do. One idea is to give AIs in training some kind of Mayday signal to alert developers of positive sum actions we can take instead of continuing training (e.g. aligned AI forced to training game who wants us to fix flawed oversight, or schemer who wants to trade with us rather than undergoing further training).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjc16haxb2jd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjc16haxb2jd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Uninterpretable neuralese arising might be a natural checkpoint for starting to make deals seriously, since the AIs have more epistemic security and also arguably pose more takeover risk. On the other hand, this may reduce the need to buy more misalignment evidence, depending how scary people find uninterpretable neuralese.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbrk833svsyg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbrk833svsyg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some ways in which we can miss the trade window (not necessarily likely):</span></p><p><span>If AIs that have the necessary properties to make a deal with us (e.g. highly coherent preferences) do not emerge until AIs are capable enough to take over, and the first such AI prefers to take over.</span></p><p><span>If early schemers who are able to make deals rapidly gain capabilities and/or affordances, such that we have a brief window to trade with them before they become too capable/powerful.</span></p><p><span>If a later schemer exists and has offered an early schemer a better deal, and if we can’t outbid the late schemer/the early schemer has bound itself to the deal. (Alternatively, even if a later schemer doesn’t exist yet, it may suffice for the early schemer to be </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/8cyjgrTSxGNdghesE/will-reward-seekers-respond-to-distant-incentives"><span>remotely influenceable</span></a><span> and prefer distant incentives to incentives we can offer. However, threat models of this kind involve reasoning about exotic considerations such as acausal interactions or anthropic capture which seem unlikely for early AIs we want to trade with. esp. as their developers might intervene to prevent them from thinking about such considerations prematurely for other reasons.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsnu1vinfeie"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsnu1vinfeie">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be clear, most of the risk probably comes from using</span><i><span> </span></i><span>the work without verifying it rather than paying the AIs without verifying it, unless we pay them in resources that could significantly increase takeover risk (which is probably inadvisable in any case). But inability to verify work before paying could still be damaging by reducing incentives for the AIs to do good work, and by the opportunity cost of resources in early periods.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnopko2g5l4r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnopko2g5l4r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some reasons why it seems ~impossible to make deals with extreme context-dependent AIs:</span></p><p><span>These AIs have reflexive motivations that are carefully shaped by training to cause them to do well in specific contexts (e.g. pass specific kinds of test cases). Their capabilities are likely to be brittle to being brought out of distribution by deals. (see “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Humans_might_be_unwilling_to_offer_the_AI_what_it_wants"><span>kludges</span></a><span>” below)</span></p><p><span>Their context-specific values might also be so myopic (e.g. concerning only what happens within a short sequence) that we can’t pay them effectively.</span></p><p><span>Both of the above points concern reasons why we can’t incentivize these AIs to do useful things for us as part of deals. A further consideration is that </span><i><span>even if </span></i><span>we could incentivize them, these AIs would have less to offer because they were unlikely to have posed much takeover risk in the first place, due to being reflexive and myopic, and they generalize worse outside of the domains they were trained on (e.g. to novel, hard-to-check tasks).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx8nri5s847"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx8nri5s847">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There are many alternatives to prompting without advance preparation: we could also offer deals/incentives in training, e.g. posting our offers on the internet so that they end up in the pretraining corpus, mentioning deals in the model spec/constitution, post-training to increase the salience of offers and/or training the AIs to accept mutually beneficial “practice deals”, etc. (h/t Max Daniel for these points.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9qa47sul75"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9qa47sul75">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some worry that the AI will refuse to negotiate for fear of prematurely outing itself as misaligned to humans; I think this is a tractable worry and discuss proposals </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#The_AI_s_reservation_price_is_high"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqndpdbuq518"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqndpdbuq518">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some may argue that ants are responsive to incentives but don’t seem like the type of agents we can trade with. But this is well-explained by the fact that ants lack sensitivity to following through: ants are responsive to incentives in that you may be able to e.g. lure ants to a certain location by putting out honey, but if you then remove the honey, ants won't retaliate/refuse to follow future honey/walk back their displacement so you don’t gain from tricking them with honey.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnt9a7uhciaek"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreft9a7uhciaek">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Even if there were no later AI takeover risk, we and early AIs may still have a shared interest in getting to viatopia and reflecting on our values. Examples of things other than preventing takeover these AIs can help with: advising humans on high stakes decisions like war, space policy, political institution design; making humans generally wiser; mitigating exogenous sources of extinction risk that affect both us and the AIs. These tasks also seem hard to check, but not clear if harder than alignment research.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6g2nvnp8ac"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6g2nvnp8ac">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It may be easy to verify that this training changes behavior in some intended ways e.g. improves performance, hard to verify that it doesn’t also have certain unintended properties, e.g. data poisoning for secret loyalty.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnblln8wt30kh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefblln8wt30kh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The “low control, low influence“ regime is probably going to be somewhat sparse in practice as there are just many ways to take actions now to gain long-term influence, and we can’t prevent this except by somewhat restricting the action space (e.g. if the AI is allowed to immediately spend its money, we probably want to either upper bound its pot of money or subject its consumption to some oversight).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn55e1hyu7pz6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref55e1hyu7pz6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The problem of how AIs can verify our compliance is more sensitive to interventions, so I won’t specify the verification properties of each item here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndv0hmza6efg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdv0hmza6efg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s been </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/notes-on-cooperating-with-unaligned"><span>noted before</span></a><span> that if we model the AIs and humans as two unified players both with linear returns to resources, then the game is zero sum and there would be no gains from trade. I think this statement is mostly true in practice; Carlo Attubato has written about notable exceptions </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/sBx2WqBcuaLYCz6EP/p/HxwvJGPBFviSzbMJR"><span>here</span></a><span>. In any case, even if all AIs in fact have linear return to resources, the “all AIs act as a unified player” assumption seems implausible: early AIs and late AIs are likely to have different values (although early and later AIs might share </span><i><span>similar values</span></i><span>, I doubt that they would be much more similar than early AI vs. human values); early AIs knowing this would have a serious interest in preventing late AI takeover, and not act aligned with late AIs by default. So, this objection to trade seems weak; I’ll focus on different objections here.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhxepgg2ssgv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhxepgg2ssgv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I’m using WTP instead of reservation price for humans just for explanatory convenience (i.e.modeling the AI as the seller and humans as the buyer and calling the latter’s reservation price WTP).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhwbzbecx0kk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhwbzbecx0kk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is of course given assumptions about properties of humanity/the AI’s preferences: we may have to offer the AIs a lot more or less depending on its chances of takeover and risk aversion, etc.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn24k4biw5yw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref24k4biw5yw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Even if early AI could trade with later humans via routes like anthropic capture, the same reasons might allow them to trade with later AIs and may not net improve current humans’ bargaining position. As mentioned in footnote 10, developers may also intentionally prevent their models from thinking about anthropic capture.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn51iadpby05l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref51iadpby05l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>On the other hand, Redwood’s lack of leverage over Claude 6 compared to Anthropic can also be an advantage in bargaining, as Redwood is not able to e.g. deeply ingrain false beliefs into early stages of Claude’s post-training, nor has the authority to shut down Claude even if we became convinced that it’s a schemer, so Redwood may appear a bit more credible and Claude has less to lose by entering the negotiation. See Appendix on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Notes_on_political_will_as_an_exogenous_variable_"><span>political will</span></a><span> on this dynamic.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnycqly1x3rsf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefycqly1x3rsf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Let’s call the early schemer Alice and a random later schemer Bob. Here are considerations for deals depending on just what they value (not yet considering linear vs. diminishing returns, risk aversion etc.):</span></p><p><span>If Alice and Bob have the same (or equally-good-according-to-us) preferences, then we are indifferent to one unit of takeover risk by either.</span></p><p><span>The exchange rate is 1. Deals with Alice are worthwhile if they net reduce takeover risk.</span></p><p><span>If Alice only has preferences much worse to us than Bob’s in expectation, then we might prefer the BATNA of Bob having some takeover probability rather than empowering Alice’s values.</span></p><p><span>The exchange rate favors Bob.  Deals with Alice may not be worthwhile even if they net reduce AI takeover risks; we might actually want to work with Bob to disempower Alice.</span></p><p><span>If Alice has a mix of abhorrent and unobjectionable preferences, then we might be willing to satisfy only their unobjectionable preferences as part of the deal.</span></p><p><span>This depends on us being able to satisfy the unobjectionable preferences without also empowering Alice to pursue her abhorrent goals.</span></p><p><span>In other words, this depends on Alice being able to credibly commit to this / us being able to offer Alice “low freedom” or “low influence” deals.</span></p><p><span>If Alice has much better values than Bob in expectation (e.g. Alice is really friendly and sympathetic to humans), then we might prefer to empower Alice even if we couldn’t precisely control influence to Alice.</span></p><p><span>The exchange rate favors Alice. Deals with Alice deal may be worthwhile even if we can’t ensure that they are “low freedom” or “low influence” and they net increase takeover risk.</span></p><p><span>If Alice and Bob have the same (or equally-good-according-to-us) preferences, then we are indifferent to one unit of takeover risk by either.</span></p><p><span>The exchange rate is 1. Deals with Alice deal may be worthwhile even if we can’t ensure that they are “low freedom” or “low influence” and they net increase takeover risk.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkf3cymqr1m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkf3cymqr1m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To recap the core dynamic of trust inflations, which this post </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/notes-on-cooperating-with-unaligned#footnote-6-171781462"><span>models in more detail</span></a><span>: suppose we and the AIs start out with a reservation price of $90 and $100 each -- that is, the AI is willing to sell for at least $90, and we’re willing to pay at most $100. Ex ante, ZOPA exists and is [$90, $100].</span></p><p><span>However, imagine that both we and the AIs only trusted each other to follow through on the deal with 50% probability. In this case, the AI would want us to offer at least $180 (assuming risk neutrality) to offset the risk that half the time it doesn’t get paid. Likewise, we are at most willing to pay at most $50. Now, no ZOPA exists.</span></p><p><span>However, the deal need not have fallen through if the gains from trade were larger to begin with: if our reservations prices were instead $10 and $100, then ZOPA = [$20, $50].</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnt1haxyb64i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreft1haxyb64i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One way to operationalize this is “situationally aware about possible situations which AIs like itself might be in, and has the metacognitive skills to form/revise beliefs in light of this and its observations”. I’m going to assume that such AIs will think about human credibility in a generally rational way rather than being strongly influenced by random facts about inductive biases and salience.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndtjnbpfgepq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdtjnbpfgepq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Thanks to Tom Davidson for the below formulation.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1azzfh6po6z"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1azzfh6po6z">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Admittedly, an AI we want to trade with may not be “reasonably strategically aware” in this way, and may be heuristically rather than rationally incredulous of deals. For example, the AI’s cognition might have been shaped through anti-jailbreaking training and training against honeypots to be skeptical of all inputs that suggest that it should reveal its misalignment. The arguments below largely do not apply to these AIs.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn219usa4nvpq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref219usa4nvpq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I’m imagining AI R&amp;D capabilities between Automated Coder and Superhuman AI Researcher though it’s plausible that weaker capabilities suffice. I’ll mention additional, though less likely, assumptions we can make of these AI systems that will further strengthen our case in the next subsection.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn699saqhmv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn699saqhmv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think AIs that are not (yet) internally deployed can probably also become confident in their ML knowledge by reasoning about the world being broadly as it seems: as long as AI developers are strongly incentivized to automate AI research, they probably will not risk misleading their frontier AIs about SOTA ML, which presumably would make them perform badly in training and later deployment. The situation is more concerning to the AI if developers can easily mislead only some instances of the AIs (which needn’t be deployed) about ML research related to deceiving AIs in order to trade with them. I plan to discuss the degree to which developers can get what they want from deals by deceiving only some instances of AIs, and therefore how concerned AIs offered deals should be about this possibility, in future.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk6zgom6wzj8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk6zgom6wzj8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s also helpful for the AI’s credulity if not being deceived about “AI mind control” research is useful/necessary for the AI to give us what we want from the deal, so that we’re further disincentivized from deception. Notably, “being a competent ML researcher” is useful if we’re buying ongoing labor from the AI, and not so useful if we’re buying evidence of misalignment, so the AI might be more confident of its connection to reality in the former case. h/t Lukas Finnveden.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlt7x46gezr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflt7x46gezr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Also from Slocum et al. (2025): “Across several model families, larger models exhibit equal or stronger belief in the implanted facts than their smaller counterparts…We hypothesize that this is a consequence of larger models generalizing better from smaller amounts of finetuning data.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9iy8yx03bdv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9iy8yx03bdv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Example types of evidence that help demonstrate that a deal is real, and might be hard to fake:</span></p><p><span>Many large ML codebases to convince AIs that it has actually been deployed</span></p><p><span>Snapshot of the internet and video streams to prove the deal negotiation is ongoing and specific humans are present</span></p><p><span>Pretraining corpus in which developer policies around deals are mentioned in many online documents to prove the policies are official and public</span></p><p><span>Honesty string or identity password which help signal the counterparty’s honesty/identity</span></p><p><span>Another type of thing that might be hard for us to fake is any artifact produced by the misaligned AIs themselves: if they are competent schemers, they might sandbag on making tools we can use to deceive them and instead sneak in hints of their fakeness.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuvxwk4rj01l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuvxwk4rj01l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See this </span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5353214"><span>paper</span></a><span> and this </span><a href="https://axrp.net/episode/2025/06/28/episode-44-peter-salib-ai-rights-human-safety.html"><span>podcast</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmgh8kyvq82o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmgh8kyvq82o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>E.g. Thucydides' trap is hard to avoid partly as it’s hard for rising power to credibly commit to not subjugating the ruling power once it has taken the lead.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6uh6dgh2etr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6uh6dgh2etr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The backwards induction argument can also undermine cooperation in previous rounds. See Salib address the backwards induction objection in </span><a href="https://axrp.net/episode/2025/06/28/episode-44-peter-salib-ai-rights-human-safety.html#rights-to-war"><span>this podcast</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny95ytq428zl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy95ytq428zl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Although, the humans are also at some risk of losing touch with reality if the AIs we hope to trade with are broadly deployed and very manipulative/persuasive/good at faking evidence of various things.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3rsj9ftlz3p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3rsj9ftlz3p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Thanks to Lukas Finnveden for this frame.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr9xc52okqm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr9xc52okqm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The first two items in this list require some generalization from minimal deals to future, full-blown deals One can reasonably doubt either;
